Completed means the initial curated cross-reference pass: the source verse and every listed target passage were read in the pinned Saints-Translation English records, and each retained pair has a specific explanation. It does not mean every possible biblical connection has been exhausted, or independent scholarly approval.

2 Kings 1:1

Moab broke faith with Israel after Ahab died.

  1. 2 Kings 3:4-5 Narrative expansion: Moab breaks with Israel after Ahab dies

    The opening notice places Moab’s break with Israel after Ahab’s death; the later account supplies Mesha’s preceding deliveries of lambs and rams and repeats the break after Ahab died.

    Read matching passage

    2 Kings 3:4 Mesha king of Moab was a noqed. At the rising, he rendered to the king of Israel a hundred thousand lambs and a hundred thousand rams in their fleeces.

    2 Kings 3:5 After Ahab died, the king of Moab broke faith with the king of Israel.

    Pinned source record

2 Kings 1:2

Ahaziah fell through the lattice in his upper room in Samaria and became ill. He sent messengers and said to them, ‘Come, and inquire of Baal Fly, the god of Ekron, whether I shall recover from this illness of mine.’ And they went to inquire through him.

  1. 2 Kings 1:16 Same-chapter literary parallel: the inquiry to Baal Fly is named in the judgment

    Ahaziah sends messengers to ask Baal Fly about recovery from his illness; Elijah’s final address expressly challenges that inquiry and announces death from the bed, linking the particular action with the oracle against it.

    Read matching passage

    2 Kings 1:16 Elijah spoke to him and said, ‘Thus says the Lord: Why did you send messengers to inquire of Baal Fly, the god of Ekron? It shall not be so! The bed onto which you have gone up—you will not come down from it, for you will surely die.’

    Pinned source record

2 Kings 1:3

An angel of the Lord spoke to Elijah the Tishbite, saying, ‘Rise, come to meet the messengers of Ahaziah king of Samaria, and say to them: Is it because there is no God in Israel that you are going to inquire of Baal Fly, the god of Ekron?’

  1. 2 Kings 1:6 Same-chapter literary parallel: the challenge reaches the king through the messengers

    The angel directs Elijah to challenge the messengers about seeking Baal Fly despite God in Israel; their report repeats that challenge to Ahaziah, with the death oracle additionally supplied.

    Read matching passage

    2 Kings 1:6 They said to him, ‘A man came up to meet us and said to us: Come, return to the king who sent you and tell him, Thus says the Lord: Is it because there is no God in Israel that you are going to inquire of Baal Fly, the god of Ekron? It shall not be so! The bed onto which you have gone up—you will not come down from it, for you will surely die.’

    Pinned source record

2 Kings 1:4

‘It shall not be so! For thus says the Lord: The bed onto which you have gone up—you will not come down from it, for there you will surely die.’ And Elijah went and spoke to them.

  1. 2 Kings 1:17 Same-chapter prophecy and fulfilment: the announced death is reported

    The Lord’s oracle through Elijah says Ahaziah will not descend from his bed but will die; the death notice expressly says he died according to the word Elijah had spoken.

    Read matching passage

    2 Kings 1:17 He died according to the word of the Lord that Elijah had spoken.

    Pinned source record

2 Kings 1:5

The messengers returned to him, and he said to them, ‘Why have you returned?’

  1. 2 Kings 1:3-4 Same-chapter narrative context: the interrupted inquiry explains the early return

    The king asks why his messengers have returned; the earlier account records Elijah’s interception and death oracle, providing the particular interruption behind the return without inventing a visit to Ekron.

    Read matching passage

    2 Kings 1:3 An angel of the Lord spoke to Elijah the Tishbite, saying, ‘Rise, come to meet the messengers of Ahaziah king of Samaria, and say to them: Is it because there is no God in Israel that you are going to inquire of Baal Fly, the god of Ekron?’

    2 Kings 1:4 ‘It shall not be so! For thus says the Lord: The bed onto which you have gone up—you will not come down from it, for there you will surely die.’ And Elijah went and spoke to them.

2 Kings 1:6

It shall not be so! The bed onto which you have gone up—you will not come down from it, for you will surely die.’

  1. 2 Kings 1:3-4 Same-chapter literary parallel: the messengers repeat the divine challenge and oracle

    Their report repeats both the challenge about God in Israel and Baal Fly and the bed-and-death warning given through Elijah; the connection distinguishes the original instruction from its reported delivery.

    Read matching passage

2 Kings 1:7

He spoke to them, saying, ‘What was the character of the man who came up to meet you and spoke these words to you?’

  1. 2 Kings 1:8 Same-chapter question and answer: identifying the messenger by appearance

    Ahaziah asks what the man who delivered the words was like; the messengers describe hairiness and a leather belt, and the king identifies Elijah the Tishbite.

    Read matching passage

    2 Kings 1:8 They said to him, ‘A hairy man, with a leather belt fastened around his waist.’ He said, ‘This is Elijah the Tishbite.’

    Pinned source record

2 Kings 1:8

They said to him, ‘A hairy man, with a leather belt fastened around his waist.’ He said, ‘This is Elijah the Tishbite.’

  1. Matthew 3:4 Descriptive parallel: hair and the leather belt in prophetic appearance

    Elijah is identified as a hairy man with a leather belt around his waist; John wears camel-hair clothing and a leather belt around his waist. The shared belt and hair imagery do not make the descriptions identical.

    Read matching passage

    Matthew 3:4 John himself wore clothing of camel’s hair and a leather belt around his waist; his food was locusts and wild honey.

    Pinned source record

2 Kings 1:9

He sent to him a commander, a captain of fifty, with his fifty men. He went up and came to him, and there Elijah was, sitting on the top of the mountain. The captain of fifty spoke to him and said, ‘Man of God, the king has summoned you. Come down!’

  1. 2 Kings 1:11 Same-chapter literary parallel: successive captains deliver the royal summons

    The first captain commands the man of God to come down at the king’s summons; the next captain repeats the command with added urgency, preserving the successive groups rather than merging them.

    Read matching passage

    2 Kings 1:11 The king again sent to him another captain of fifty with his fifty men. The captain went up and spoke to him, saying, ‘Man of God, thus says the king: Come down quickly!’

    Pinned source record

2 Kings 1:10

Elijah answered and said to the captain of fifty, ‘If I am a man of God, fire will come down from heaven and consume you and your fifty men.’ Fire came down from heaven and consumed him and his fifty men.

  1. 2 Kings 1:12 Same-chapter literary parallel: repeated fire upon two separate companies

    Elijah answers the first captain with the conditional man-of-God declaration and fire consumes his company; the second company receives the corresponding declaration and fire. Each company remains a separate narrated occurrence.

    Read matching passage

    2 Kings 1:12 Elijah answered and spoke to him, saying, ‘If I am a man of God, fire will come down from heaven and consume you and your fifty men.’ Fire came down from heaven and consumed him and his fifty men.

    Pinned source record

  2. Luke 9:54-55 Verbal and narrative allusion: fire from heaven to consume people

    Elijah announces heavenly fire that consumes the captain and fifty men; James and John propose commanding heavenly fire to consume the unwelcoming villagers, and Jesus rebukes them. Luke records a rejected proposal rather than another performed destruction.

    Read matching passage

    Luke 9:54 Seeing this, the disciples James and John said, “Lord, dost Thou wish us to command fire to come down from heaven and consume them?”

    Luke 9:55 But He turned and rebuked them.

    Pinned source record

2 Kings 1:11

The king again sent to him another captain of fifty with his fifty men. The captain went up and spoke to him, saying, ‘Man of God, thus says the king: Come down quickly!’

  1. 2 Kings 1:9 Same-chapter literary parallel: the second royal command repeats the first

    The second captain tells the man of God to come down quickly; the first captain likewise delivered the king’s demand to come down, establishing the repeated command while retaining the second command’s added urgency.

    Read matching passage

    2 Kings 1:9 He sent to him a commander, a captain of fifty, with his fifty men. He went up and came to him, and there Elijah was, sitting on the top of the mountain. The captain of fifty spoke to him and said, ‘Man of God, the king has summoned you. Come down!’

    Pinned source record

2 Kings 1:12

Elijah answered and spoke to him, saying, ‘If I am a man of God, fire will come down from heaven and consume you and your fifty men.’ Fire came down from heaven and consumed him and his fifty men.

  1. Sirach 48:3 Later biblical recollection: Elijah and descending fire

    The second captain and his fifty are consumed by fire from heaven after Elijah speaks; Sirach recalls Elijah bringing down fire three times by the Lord’s word, without identifying every individual occurrence in that summary.

    Read matching passage

    Sirach 48:3 By the Lord’s word he held back heaven; in the same way he brought down fire three times.

    Pinned source record

2 Kings 1:13

The king again sent a commander, a third captain of fifty, with his fifty men. The third captain of fifty came to him, bent his knees before Elijah, and pleaded with him, saying, ‘Man of God, please let my life and the lives of these fifty slaves of yours be honored in your eyes.’

  1. 2 Kings 1:9-12 Same-chapter narrative contrast: petition replaces the earlier captains’ commands

    The third captain kneels and asks that his and his men’s lives be honored; the first two captains issued royal commands and their companies were consumed. The different forms of address and stated outcomes remain distinct.

2 Kings 1:14

‘Behold, fire came down from heaven and consumed the first two captains of fifty and their fifty men. But now please let the lives of your slaves be honored in your eyes.’

  1. 2 Kings 1:10-12 Same-chapter retrospective reference: the third captain recalls the earlier fire

    The third captain explicitly recalls fire consuming the first two captains and their men; the preceding account narrates those two destructions separately, grounding the particular danger invoked in his plea.

2 Kings 1:15

An angel of the Lord spoke to Elijah and said, ‘Go down with him; do not be afraid of them.’ Elijah rose and went down with him to the king.

  1. 2 Kings 1:9 Same-chapter command contrast: the angel authorizes Elijah’s descent

    The angel tells Elijah to go down without fear, and he goes to the king; the first captain had already demanded descent under royal authority. The link marks the different speakers and Elijah’s reported response to the angel.

    Read matching passage

    2 Kings 1:9 He sent to him a commander, a captain of fifty, with his fifty men. He went up and came to him, and there Elijah was, sitting on the top of the mountain. The captain of fifty spoke to him and said, ‘Man of God, the king has summoned you. Come down!’

Elijah spoke to him and said, ‘Thus says the Lord: Why did you send messengers to inquire of Baal Fly, the god of Ekron? It shall not be so! The bed onto which you have gone up—you will not come down from it, for you will surely die.’

  1. 2 Kings 1:3-4 Same-chapter literary parallel: Elijah repeats the inquiry-and-death oracle directly

    Elijah’s address to the king again names the inquiry to Baal Fly and the warning that he will not descend from the bed but die, repeating the message initially given through the intercepted messengers.

2 Kings 1:17

He died according to the word of the Lord that Elijah had spoken.

  1. 2 Kings 1:4 Same-chapter prophecy and fulfilment: the narrator attributes death to the prior word

    The death notice expressly connects Ahaziah’s death with Elijah’s word; the earlier oracle supplies the particular bed-and-death warning, without adding an independently diagnosed medical cause.

    Read matching passage

    2 Kings 1:4 ‘It shall not be so! For thus says the Lord: The bed onto which you have gone up—you will not come down from it, for there you will surely die.’ And Elijah went and spoke to them.

    Pinned source record

  2. Sirach 48:6 Later biblical recollection: Elijah’s judgments upon kings and men in bed

    Ahaziah dies according to Elijah’s word after the bed oracle; Sirach praises Elijah for bringing kings to destruction and glorious men from their bed. The thematic royal-and-bed connection does not insert Ahaziah’s name into Sirach.

    Read matching passage

    Sirach 48:6 You brought kings down to destruction and glorious men from their bed.

    Pinned source record

2 Kings 1:18

The rest of the acts of Ahaziah, all that he did—behold, are these not written in the book of the daily records for the kings of Israel?

  1. 1 Kings 22:52-54 Regnal narrative framing: Ahaziah’s opening and closing notices

    The closing notice refers Ahaziah’s remaining acts to Israel’s royal daily records; the earlier regnal notice supplies his parentage, Samarian rule and conduct. This links the named king’s narrative notices without claiming the cited annals were read.

    Read matching passage

    1 Kings 22:52 Ahaziah son of Ahab became king over Israel in Samaria in the seventeenth year of Jehoshaphat king of Judah, and he reigned in Israel two years.

    1 Kings 22:53 He did evil before the Lord and walked in the way of Ahab his father, the way of Jezebel his mother, and the sins of the house of Jeroboam son of Nebat, who caused Israel to sin.

    1 Kings 22:54 He served the Baalim, bowed down to them, and provoked the Lord, the God of Israel, according to all that had happened before him.

    Pinned source record

2 Kings 1:18a

Jehoram son of Ahab reigns over Israel in Samaria for twelve years, beginning in the eighteenth year of Jehoshaphat king of Judah.

  1. 2 Kings 3:1 Repeated regnal notice: Jehoram’s parentage and twelve-year reign

    Both notices name Jehoram son of Ahab ruling Israel for twelve years from Jehoshaphat’s eighteenth year. The continuation additionally names Samaria, preserving that detail without inserting it into the later notice.

    Read matching passage

    2 Kings 3:1 Jehoram son of Ahab became king in Israel in the eighteenth year of Jehoshaphat king of Judah, and he reigned twelve years.

    Pinned source record

2 Kings 1:18b

He did what was evil before the Lord, though not as his brothers did, nor as his mother did.

  1. 2 Kings 3:2 Repeated regnal assessment with a stated family-comparison difference

    Both call Jehoram’s conduct evil and qualify the comparison with his mother; the continuation compares him with his brothers, whereas the later notice compares him with his father. Those different family clauses remain unresolved rather than equivalent.

    Read matching passage

    2 Kings 3:2 He did what was evil in the eyes of the Lord, though not as his father and not as his mother had done. He removed the pillars of Baal that his father had made.

    Pinned source record

2 Kings 1:18c

He removed the pillars of Baal that his father had made and shattered them. Yet he clung to the sins of the house of Jeroboam, who caused Israel to sin; he did not turn away from them.

  1. 2 Kings 3:2-3 Repeated regnal assessment: Baal pillars removed but Jeroboam’s sin retained

    The continuation combines removal and shattering of the father’s Baal pillars with continued adherence to Jeroboam’s sins; the later notices likewise report removal and continued adherence, without adding the shattering detail to them.

    Read matching passage

    2 Kings 3:2 He did what was evil in the eyes of the Lord, though not as his father and not as his mother had done. He removed the pillars of Baal that his father had made.

    2 Kings 3:3 Yet he clung to the sin of Jeroboam son of Nebat, who caused Israel to sin; he did not turn away from it.

    Pinned source record

2 Kings 1:18d

The Lord was stirred to anger against the house of Ahab.

  1. 1 Kings 20:21-22 Prophetic judgment and later dynastic assessment: Ahab’s house

    The continuation states that the Lord was angered against Ahab’s house; Elijah’s earlier oracle names calamities against Ahab and his house because of the provocations and Israel’s sin, supplying the particular dynastic judgment rather than another house’s oracle.

    Read matching passage

    1 Kings 20:21 Thus says the Lord: ‘Behold, I am bringing calamities upon you. I will burn out what remains after you and destroy from Ahab every male who urinates against a wall, both confined and abandoned in Israel.

    1 Kings 20:22 I will make your house like the house of Jeroboam son of Nebat and like the house of Baasha son of Ahijah, because of the provocations you have provoked and because you did cause Israel to sin.’”

    Pinned source record

2 Kings 2:1

When the Lord was about to take Elijah up in a tumult, as into heaven, Elijah and Elisha went from Gilgal.

  1. Sirach 48:12 Later biblical recollection: Elijah’s whirlwind and Elisha’s succession

    Kings introduces the Lord taking Elijah up in a tumult, as into heaven, while Elisha travels with him; Sirach recalls Elijah covered by a whirlwind and Elisha filled with his spirit, preserving each account’s wording.

    Read matching passage

    Sirach 48:12 Elijah was covered by a whirlwind, and Elisha was filled with his spirit. In his days he was not shaken by a ruler, and no one overpowered him.

    Pinned source record

2 Kings 2:2

Elijah said to Elisha, ‘Please stay here, for the Lord has sent me as far as Bethel.’ Elisha said, ‘As the Lord lives, and as you live, I will not leave you.’ So they came to Bethel.

  1. 2 Kings 2:4 Same-chapter literary parallel: repeated refusal to leave Elijah

    At the Bethel stage Elisha swears by the Lord’s life and Elijah’s life that he will not leave; the Jericho stage repeats that pledge, with the different destinations retained.

    Read matching passage

    2 Kings 2:4 Elijah said to Elisha, ‘Please stay here, for the Lord has sent me to Jericho.’ Elisha said, ‘As the Lord lives, and as you live, I will not leave you.’ So they came to Jericho.

    Pinned source record

2 Kings 2:3

The sons of the prophets who were in Bethel came to Elisha and said to him, ‘Do you know that this day the Lord is taking your master away from above your head?’ He said, ‘I too know it. Be silent.’

  1. 2 Kings 2:5 Same-chapter literary parallel: prophetic groups announce the same impending removal

    The sons of the prophets at Bethel tell Elisha his master is being taken that day and receive his knowing reply; the Jericho group repeats the announcement and reply, without merging the two groups.

    Read matching passage

    2 Kings 2:5 The sons of the prophets who were in Jericho approached Elisha and said to him, ‘Do you know that this day the Lord is taking your master away from above your head?’ He said, ‘Indeed, I too know it. Be silent.’

    Pinned source record

2 Kings 2:4

Elijah said to Elisha, ‘Please stay here, for the Lord has sent me to Jericho.’ Elisha said, ‘As the Lord lives, and as you live, I will not leave you.’ So they came to Jericho.

  1. 2 Kings 2:6 Same-chapter literary parallel: the loyalty pledge persists toward the Jordan

    Elisha refuses to remain behind on the Jericho stage; he repeats the Lord-and-master life oath when Elijah names the Jordan, showing the repeated refusal at successive destinations.

    Read matching passage

    2 Kings 2:6 Elijah said to him, ‘Please stay here, for the Lord has sent me as far as the Jordan.’ Elisha said, ‘As the Lord lives, and as you live, I will not leave you.’ So both of them went on.

    Pinned source record

2 Kings 2:5

The sons of the prophets who were in Jericho approached Elisha and said to him, ‘Do you know that this day the Lord is taking your master away from above your head?’ He said, ‘Indeed, I too know it. Be silent.’

  1. 2 Kings 2:3 Same-chapter literary parallel: Jericho’s warning corresponds to Bethel’s warning

    The Jericho prophetic sons announce that the Lord is taking Elisha’s master away that day; the Bethel group had given the corresponding announcement, and Elisha tells both that he knows and asks silence.

    Read matching passage

    2 Kings 2:3 The sons of the prophets who were in Bethel came to Elisha and said to him, ‘Do you know that this day the Lord is taking your master away from above your head?’ He said, ‘I too know it. Be silent.’

    Pinned source record

2 Kings 2:6

Elijah said to him, ‘Please stay here, for the Lord has sent me as far as the Jordan.’ Elisha said, ‘As the Lord lives, and as you live, I will not leave you.’ So both of them went on.

  1. 2 Kings 4:30 Narrative verbal parallel: the same life oath and refusal to leave

    Elisha swears by the Lord’s life and Elijah’s life that he will not leave him; the child’s mother later uses the corresponding oath and refusal toward Elisha, with different speakers and circumstances preserved.

    Read matching passage

    2 Kings 4:30 The child’s mother said, ‘As the Lord lives, and as you live, I will not leave you.’ Elisha rose and went behind her.

2 Kings 2:7

Fifty men, sons of the prophets, stood opposite them at a distance, while both of them stood beside the Jordan.

  1. 2 Kings 2:15 Same-chapter narrative continuity: prophetic witnesses across from the Jordan

    Fifty prophetic sons stand opposite at a distance while Elijah and Elisha stand by the Jordan; the later notice places Jericho’s prophetic sons opposite Elisha as they recognize Elijah’s spirit upon him, without enumerating each returning witness.

    Read matching passage

    2 Kings 2:15 The sons of the prophets in Jericho saw him from opposite him and said, ‘The spirit of Elijah has come to rest upon Elisha.’ They came to meet him and bowed before him to the ground.

    Pinned source record

2 Kings 2:8

Elijah took his sheepskin, rolled it up, and struck the water. The water divided to this side and that, and both of them crossed over on barren ground.

  1. Joshua 3:15-17 Narrative parallel: the Jordan permits a dry crossing

    Elijah strikes the Jordan with his rolled sheepskin, the water divides, and the two cross on barren ground; Joshua describes the Jordan stopping and Israel crossing on dry ground with the ark’s priests. Actors and means remain different.

    Read matching passage

    Joshua 3:15 As the priests carrying the ark of the covenant entered the Jordan and the feet of the priests carrying the ark of the covenant of the Lord dipped into the edge of the Jordan’s water—the Jordan was overflowing its whole bank, as in the days of wheat harvest—

    Joshua 3:16 the waters coming down from above stood. One mass stood very, very far away, as far as the region of Kiriath-jearim. The water flowing down descended into the Sea of Arabah, the Salt Sea, until it failed completely. The people stood opposite Jericho.

    Joshua 3:17 The priests carrying the ark of the covenant of the Lord stood on dry ground in the middle of the Jordan. All the sons of Israel crossed on dry ground until the whole people had finished crossing the Jordan.

    Pinned source record

2 Kings 2:9

When they had crossed, Elijah said to Elisha, ‘Ask what I should do for you before I am taken up from you.’ Elisha said, ‘Please let a double portion of your spirit be upon me.’

  1. Deuteronomy 21:17 Lexical and inheritance imagery: the double portion

    Elisha asks for a double portion of Elijah’s spirit; Deuteronomy gives the acknowledged firstborn a double portion of possessions. The shared inheritance expression does not establish a legal adoption or quantify Elisha’s miracles.

    Read matching passage

    Deuteronomy 21:17 But he shall acknowledge the firstborn, the son of the hated wife, by giving him a double portion of all that he has; for he is the beginning of his children, and the right of the firstborn belongs unto him.

    Pinned source record

  2. 2 Kings 2:15 Same-chapter request and subsequent recognition: Elijah’s spirit upon Elisha

    Elisha requests a double portion of Elijah’s spirit; the prophetic sons later say Elijah’s spirit rests upon him. Their statement recognizes the spirit without explicitly measuring it as double.

    Read matching passage

    2 Kings 2:15 The sons of the prophets in Jericho saw him from opposite him and said, ‘The spirit of Elijah has come to rest upon Elisha.’ They came to meet him and bowed before him to the ground.

    Pinned source record

2 Kings 2:10

Elijah said, ‘You have made a difficult request. If you see me being taken up from you, it will be so for you; but if not, it certainly will not happen.’

  1. 2 Kings 2:12 Same-chapter condition and narrated observation: seeing Elijah’s removal

    Elijah makes seeing him taken away the condition attached to Elisha’s request; Elisha is then described watching and crying out before seeing him no more, connecting the stated condition with the narrated sight.

    Read matching passage

    2 Kings 2:12 Elisha was watching and crying, ‘Father, father! Israel’s chariot and its horseman!’ He saw him no more. He seized his clothes and tore them into two pieces.

    Pinned source record

2 Kings 2:11

As they went, walking and speaking, behold—a chariot of fire and horses of fire separated the two of them, and Elijah was taken up in a tumult, as into heaven.

  1. Sirach 48:9 Later biblical recollection: fiery chariot and horses at Elijah’s removal

    Kings says the fiery chariot and horses separate the two men as Elijah is taken in a tumult, as into heaven; Sirach recalls his fiery whirlwind, chariot and fiery horses. It does not supply Kings with an unstated riding action.

    Read matching passage

    Sirach 48:9 You were taken up in a whirlwind of fire, in a chariot with fiery horses.

    Pinned source record

2 Kings 2:12

Elisha was watching and crying, ‘Father, father! Israel’s chariot and its horseman!’ He saw him no more. He seized his clothes and tore them into two pieces.

  1. 2 Kings 13:14 Verbal narrative parallel: father and Israel’s chariot and horseman

    Elisha cries Father and Israel’s chariot and horseman as Elijah is removed; Joash later uses that corresponding address while weeping over the dying Elisha, preserving the different speaker, prophet and circumstances.

    Read matching passage

    2 Kings 13:14 Elisha fell ill with the sickness from which he died. Joash king of Israel came down to him, wept over his face, and said, ‘Father, father! Israel’s chariot and its horseman!’

    Pinned source record

  2. 2 Kings 2:10 Same-chapter narrative observation and earlier condition

    Elisha is watching as he cries out and then sees Elijah no more; Elijah had attached the promised outcome to Elisha seeing him taken away, rather than merely hearing about it afterward.

    Read matching passage

    2 Kings 2:10 Elijah said, ‘You have made a difficult request. If you see me being taken up from you, it will be so for you; but if not, it certainly will not happen.’

    Pinned source record

2 Kings 2:13

He picked up Elijah’s sheepskin, which had fallen upon Elisha, and Elisha returned and stood on the edge of the Jordan.

  1. 1 Kings 19:19 Narrative object continuity: Elijah’s sheepskin and Elisha’s calling

    Elisha picks up Elijah’s sheepskin, which the text describes as having fallen upon Elisha; the calling account had Elijah throw his sheepskin cloak over Elisha. The shared object and named relationship connect calling and succession without adding a new anointing scene.

Pour all blood of whole burnt offering and all blood of sacrifice against it. The bronze altar shall be for me for the morning.’

  1. 2 Kings 16:16 Same-chapter command and compliance: Uriah performs Ahaz’s instructions

    Ahaz specifies the offerings on the great altar and reserves the bronze altar for himself for the morning; the following notice says Uriah did everything commanded, without independently changing that reservation’s wording.

    Read matching passage

    2 Kings 16:16 Uriah the priest did in accordance with everything King Ahaz commanded him.

  1. 1 Kings 7:13 Temple-object continuity: the Sea and its bronze oxen

    Ahaz takes the Sea off its bronze oxen and places it on stone; the construction account describes twelve cattle beneath the Sea, with the Sea above them. The particular earlier arrangement is linked without independently translating mechonoth or adding a destruction of the oxen.

  1. 2 Kings 16:7-10 Same-chapter narrative context: temple alterations and the Assyrian king

    The entrance alteration is expressly because of Assyria’s king; the earlier notices narrate Ahaz’s appeal, gifts, the Damascus capture and meeting that king. The link does not independently explain the seat’s foundation or the king’s private motive.

2 Kings 17:7

  1. Exodus 20:2-5 Covenantal contrast: the Exodus God and worship of other gods

    Kings attributes Israel’s fall to sin against the Lord who brought them out of Egypt and to fearing other gods; the commandments name that deliverance and forbid other gods, images and bowing to them.

  1. Deuteronomy 18:9-12 Covenantal contrast: adopting the displaced nations’ practices

    Kings says Israel followed the nations’ ordinances; Deuteronomy forbids learning their abominations and describes their removal because of such practices. The law’s particular examples are not inserted into this incomplete-running sentence.

  1. Jeremiah 25:4-5 Prophetic summons parallel: servants call for turning from evil

    Kings summarizes the Lord’s prophets calling Israel and Judah to turn from evil and keep His commands; the Jeremiah passage likewise uses the language of sending prophetic servants with the summons to turn. The connection does not identify every prophet in Kings.

  1. Jeremiah 2:5 Verbal prophetic parallel: following vanity and becoming vain

    Kings says the people followed vain things and became vain; Jeremiah asks why the fathers withdrew, followed futile things and became futile. The shared pursuit-and-result wording is retained without identifying a single historical audience.

    Kings connects abandoned testimony, vanity and imitation of nations; Romans describes people becoming futile in thought and exchanging God’s glory for images. The parallel does not make Paul explicitly cite this Kings passage.
